# Merged Evidence Register — all 20 streams

**Built:** 2026-10-10 (assembly) · **Research date:** 2026-10-10 · **Scope:** every finding from all 20 workstreams' evidence registers, one row per finding, organized by report section (1–19 + appendices).

**Merge stats:** 1,092 findings extracted → **1,081 rows** after 11 curated merges (same finding across streams → one row, corroboration noted on the surviving row). No finding dropped: every extracted finding appears exactly once below or is folded into a surviving row with its stream/ID cited.

**Labels:** Verified / Corroborated / Inferred / Proposed / Unknown — preserved exactly as assigned by the research; nothing upgraded. Where a source register did not assign a label, the cell shows `—` and the confidence field is preserved verbatim; no label was invented.

**Reading a row:** ID = `stream/original-ID` (synthetic IDs like `EX-001` or `discovery-001` were assigned only where the source register had no ID column). Claims are condensed; full per-finding detail lives in the source register file named in the Section intro.

**Sections with no dedicated rows:** §1 (executive findings — synthesis across all rows below), §16 (implementation roadmap — entirely Proposed synthesis), §17 (checklists/templates — draws on the rules, discovery, and quality rows in their home sections), §18 (report-level evidence reading guide — points here). §§4–6 were not visible at assembly time; streams that likely feed them are noted per section.

---

## §3 — The Complete Product Lifecycle

*Basis: discovery (confirmed — lifecycle discovery stages; §§16–17 also draw on discovery checklists). Rows: 40.*

| ID | Finding | Label | Source | Type / Author | Pub | Confidence | Corroboration / Contradictions | Implication |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| discovery/A1 | Continuous discovery = at minimum weekly customer touchpoints by the team building the product; decisions framed as compare-and-contrast, never single-option yes/no | Corroborated | https://github.com/audenaert/etak/blob/HEAD/docs/profiles/teresa-torres.md ;… | Secondary interpretation (skill/profile docs summarizing Continuous Discovery… | Book 2021; docs 2026 | Medium-high | Corrob: Consistent across 4+ independent skill docs Contra: Summaries, not the book itself; exact "weekly" cadence is Torres's stated minimum, teams adapt it | Adopt weekly touchpoint cadence as the discovery heartbeat; enforce multi-option comparison in reviews |
| discovery/A2 | Opportunity Solution Tree: outcome → opportunities → solutions → experiments; a thinking tool, not documentation; anti-patterns include solution-first framing and treating it as a roadmap | Corroborated | https://github.com/prorise-cool/prorise-claude-skills/blob/HEAD/.claude/skills/product-spe… | Secondary interpretation; Torres (via community) | 2026 (doc) | Medium-high | Corrob: 5+ near-identical community docs Contra: Community consensus may smooth over nuances of the original | Use OST to trace every idea to an outcome; reject ideas that can't be traced |
| discovery/A3 | Torres rejects quantified scoring formulas for opportunity selection ("messy, subjective decisions"); treats them as reversible two-way-door decisions where speed beats false precision | Corroborated | https://github.com/tannerbroberts/ost-agent/blob/HEAD/docs/reference/teresa-torres-ost.md | Secondary interpretation; Torres (via summarizer) | 2026 (doc) | Medium | Corrob: Consistent with independent false-precision critiques (B4, B5) Contra: Single secondary source for the direct quote; treat as attributed, not verbatim-verified | Prefer fast assumption tests over scoring rituals for reversible decisions |
| discovery/A4 | Product discovery = build to learn (cheap prototypes, 10–20+/week on strong teams); delivery = build to earn; the four risks are value, usability, feasibility, business viability — attack early, cheaply, together | Corroborated | https://github.com/marcos-sponton/frameworks-as-skills/blob/HEAD/_research/marty-cagan.md… | Secondary interpretation (SVPG/Cagan); Marty Cagan / SVPG (via summarizers) | Books 2008–2024; docs… | High | Corrob: 5+ independent summaries agree on the four risks verbatim Contra: Summaries, not SVPG primary pages (svpg.com not fetched in this pass) | Staff discovery with the product trio; prototype before production code; assign risk owners |
| discovery/A5 | AI collapsing delivery cost shifts the bottleneck to discovery — "which is exactly what most orgs are worst at" (Cagan, 2026) | Corroborated | https://github.com/marcos-sponton/frameworks-as-skills/blob/HEAD/_research/marty-cagan.md… | Secondary interpretation; Cagan (via researcher) | 2026-04-16 (cited) | Medium | Corrob: Directionally consistent with AI-discovery literature (A12) Contra: Citation chain: skill doc → svpg.com; primary page not fetched | Invest discovery capacity as AI lowers build cost; don't mistake faster building for less risk |
| discovery/A6 | Customer development: startups search for a repeatable business model via Customer Discovery → Validation → Creation → Company Building; "get out of the building"; pivot when hypotheses are invalidated | Corroborated | https://github.com/nmor/the-claude-council/blob/HEAD/skills/lean-startup/SKILL.md ;… | Secondary interpretation; Steve Blank (via community) | Book 2005; docs 2026 | High | Corrob: Consistent across 4+ docs incl. pivot-type taxonomy Contra: None material; widely taught | Run problem→solution→product interview sequence; define pivot triggers upfront |
| discovery/A7 | Mom Test rules: talk about their life not your idea; ask about specific past behavior not hypotheticals; compliments/fluff = bad data; seek commitment & advancement | Corroborated | https://github.com/ycanerden/habitat-skills/blob/HEAD/skills/idea-validation/SKILL.md ;… | Secondary interpretation; Rob Fitzpatrick (via community) | Book 2013; docs 2026 | High | Corrob: Consistent across 3+ docs Contra: None | Script interviews around past behavior; disqualify compliment-only evidence |

## §20 — Compulsory Disciplines (follow-up phase)

*Basis: discipline-trust-safety, discipline-ai-act, discipline-billing, discipline-mobile-release, discipline-notifications, discipline-api-product, discipline-compliance, discipline-finops, discipline-blindspots (researched 2026-10-10). Rows: 372. Labels preserved exactly as assigned by the research; nothing upgraded.*

| ID | Finding | Label | Source | Type / Author | Pub | Confidence | Corroboration / Contradictions | Implication |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ai-act/AIACT-001 | AI Act entered into force 1 Aug 2024 and phases in via Art 113: prohibitions + AI literacy 2 Feb 2025; GPAI + governance 2 Aug 2025; remaining provisions (incl. high-risk) on later dates; transitional rules Art 111 for legacy systems | **Verified** | https://github.com/koskinep/eu-ai-act-skill/blob/HEAD/SKILL.md | Open-source EU AI Act skill repo (engineering reference) | Apr 2026 (status notes) | Original Art 113 dates superseded for high-risk tiers by the Digital Omnibus; this source's status notes (Apr 2026) predate final adoption (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 text (Art 113) and all timeline trackers | Engineering plans must track which obligations are live vs deferred — a single "compliance deadline" framing is wrong |
| ai-act/AIACT-002 | Digital Omnibus on AI (Regulation (EU) 2026/1744): EP adopted 16 Jun 2026, Council 29 Jun 2026, OJ published 24 Jul 2026, in force 27 Jul 2026; moves Annex III high-risk to 2 Dec 2027 and Annex I product-embedded to 2 Aug 2028; transparency, prohibitions unchanged | **Corroborated** | https://www.regulation-ai.eu/en/blog/eu-ai-act-enforcement-august-2026/ ; https://www.techtimes.com/articles/320101/20260710/eu-ai-act-enforcement-here-chatbot-rules-live-high-risk-ai-delay-now-binding-law.htm | EU AI Act policy tracker (regulation-ai.eu); tech news (Tech Times) | Aug 2026; Jul 2026 | OJ dates per CMS analysis cited in secondary sources; political-deal date (May–Jun 2026) varies slightly by source (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by quantamixsolutions.com, mindoxai.com timelines and jdsupra analysis of the amendment | Treat Omnibus dates as binding only as reflected in the consolidated EUR-Lex text; any engineering plan built on pre-July 2026 material carries the original (wrong) high-risk dates |
| ai-act/AIACT-003 | Article 50 transparency obligations enforceable since 2 Aug 2026: chatbot/AI-interaction disclosure (50(1)); machine-readable marking of synthetic content (50(2)); emotion-recognition/biometric-categorisation notice (50(3)); deepfake and public-interest AI-text labelling (50(4)) | **Verified** | https://www.regulation-ai.eu/en/blog/eu-ai-act-enforcement-august-2026/ | EU AI Act policy tracker (regulation-ai.eu) | Sep 2026 | Stated directly from the Regulation's provisions; four-sub-obligation structure matches the Act's text (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by law.com, newscase.com, ewow.news, vestbee.com, technology.org, CSA lab note | Every EU-facing conversational or generative system needs disclosure/marking components now, regardless of risk tier |
| ai-act/AIACT-004 | 4-month grace period (to 2 Dec 2026) for Article 50(2) machine-readable marking applies ONLY to generative systems already on the EU market before 2 Aug 2026; all other Art 50 duties had no grace period; Commission AI Board (9th plenary, 17 Sep 2026) granted no further relief | **Verified** | https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-eu-ai-act-article50-watermarking-deadline/ | CSA research note | Sep 2026 | Narrow legal reading confirmed by the AI Board's public materials (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by regulation-ai.eu, ewow.news, techtimes.com | Retrofit watermarking/metadata into existing generative systems before 2 Dec 2026 or withdraw them from the EU market |
| ai-act/AIACT-005 | New prohibition added by the Digital Omnibus: AI-generated CSAM and non-consensual intimate imagery ("nudification") — applicable from 2 Dec 2026 | **Corroborated** | https://github.com/karunmehta-aigp/ai-governance/blob/HEAD/reference-library/ai-law-and-standards-reference.md ; https://github.com/botgenossen/eu-ai-act-implementation-guide | Practitioner AI-governance reference library; community implementation guide (botgenossen GmbH) | Jun–Aug 2026 | Sourced from Omnibus amendment tracking; not yet independently verified against the OJ amending text by this research (accessed 2026-10-10) | Single legal-text origin (Omnibus amends Art 5); no contradictions found | Any generative image/video system needs output-side abuse filters covering CSAM and non-consensual intimate imagery before 2 Dec 2026 |
| ai-act/AIACT-006 | Three-tier fine structure (Art 99): €35M or 7% worldwide turnover (prohibited practices); €15M or 3% (high-risk/GPAI/transparency); €7.5M or 1.5% (misleading info to authorities); percentages use the HIGHER for large orgs, LOWER for SMEs/startups (Art 99(6)) | **Verified** | https://www.legalithm.com/en/blog/eu-ai-act-penalties-fines-explained | Legal-tech analysis (Legalithm) | Aug 2026 | Direct from Art 99; tier-3 percentage stated as 1% in some sources, 1.5% in others — minor discrepancy flagged (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by axis-intelligence.com, kurums.com, vestbee.com, informedclearly.com, regulation-ai.eu | Fine tiers are the compulsory backstop; the 7% ceiling exceeds GDPR's 4%, making this the harshest EU digital-reg penalty regime by maximum |
| ai-act/AIACT-007 | Prohibited practices enforceable since 2 Feb 2025 with the Commission having launched initial investigations; no publicly reported AI Act fine located as of Oct 2026 | **Corroborated** (investigations) / **Unknown** (first fine) | https://www.legalithm.com/en/blog/eu-ai-act-penalties-fines-explained | Legal analysis | Aug 2026 | Claim of "initial investigations" is single-sourced and vague; no named enforcement action found across this research (accessed 2026-10-10) | No contradictory evidence found; absence of fines is consistent with the enforcement-buildup timeline | Enforcement is live in powers but unobserved in penalties — teams should not infer laxity from the absence of fines |
| ai-act/AIACT-008 | Since 2 Aug 2026: Commission AI Office holds enforcement powers over GPAI providers (request docs, evaluate models, order corrective measures, impose fines up to €15M/3% — retroactively for violations back to Aug 2025); AI Office assembled 38-person enforcement team; enforcement commencement announced 31 Jul 2026 | **Corroborated** | https://news.outsourceaccelerator.com/eu-ai-act-enforcement/ ; https://axis-intelligence.com/eu-ai-act-compliance-tracker/ | Tech news (citing Commission); compliance tracker | Aug 2026 | Team size (38) is single-sourced; retroactive fining power follows from Art 101 application date + Art 99/101 structure (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by techtimes.com, linkedin analysis | Model providers must assume documentation and copyright-policy compliance is already examinable, including for the Aug 2025–Aug 2026 period |
| ai-act/AIACT-009 | Act applies extraterritorially: providers and deployers in third countries are covered when their AI system's output is used in the EU (Art 2(1)(c)); a single EU client relationship triggers coverage | **Verified** (legal text) | https://casrai.org/guides/ai-regulations-around-the-world | Global AI-regulation survey (CASRAI) | Sep 2026 | Legal-text claim confirmed by multiple practitioner guides (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by linkedin analysis, informedclearly.com | Non-EU engineering teams cannot treat the Act as out-of-scope; US/Asia SaaS vendors owe Art 50 duties now if outputs reach EU users |
| ai-act/AIACT-010 | Implementation readiness uneven: ~10 of 27 member states show advanced public evidence (Ireland, Spain, Lithuania, Finland, France, Germany, Netherlands, Poland, Cyprus, Italy); 17 have limited public footprints — enforcement-arbitrage risk | **Corroborated** | https://informedclearly.com/en/ai/55795/eu-ai-act-compliance-deadline-2026 | Compliance analysis citing Alice Labs EU AI Act Implementation Tracker 2026 (Jun 26) | Jun–Aug 2026 | Tertiary reporting of a tracker; "public evidence" ≠ actual authority capacity; directionally credible but not independently audited here (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by axis-intelligence.com tracker | Route governance evidence through the strongest member-state authorities; do not assume uniform interpretation |
| ai-act/AIACT-011 | Corporate readiness gap: 78% of enterprises took no meaningful compliance steps (Feb 2026 report); 83% lack systematic AI inventory; compliance cost estimates $500k–$2M (SMEs) / $8–15M (large enterprises) | **Corroborated** (at secondary source) | https://informedclearly.com/en/ai/63429/eu-ai-act-compliance-companies-unprepared-2026 | Compliance journalism aggregating EU AI Act statistics | Sep 2026 | Secondary aggregation; original Feb 2026 report not directly inspected — treat as directional, not precise (accessed 2026-10-10) | No contradicting figures found; consistent with the "boardroom comfort" narrative | The inventory is step zero; classification cannot start without knowing what AI the organisation runs |
| ai-act/AIACT-012 | Risk classification is a two-route test under Art 6: Annex I (product-safety/safety-component) and Annex III (eight use domains); Art 6(3) narrow-task exemption NEVER applies to systems profiling natural persons and must be documented/registered | **Verified** | https://github.com/botgenossen/eu-ai-act-implementation-guide/blob/HEAD/README.md | Community implementation guide (botgenossen GmbH) | Aug 2026 | Direct from the Act's text; the Omnibus simplified Art 6(3) registration (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by mchittineni/ultimate-ai-engineering-guide, suacalis/claude-skills, hermes-mcp compliance framework | Implement classification as a versioned decision-tree record per system; treat Art 6(3) claims as high-scrutiny assertions, not self-declared loopholes |
| ai-act/AIACT-013 | Classification-by-design: a benefits-policy assistant stays out of high-risk because it never outputs an eligibility determination — enforced by an output guard, citation requirement, and a refusal eval suite; architecture IS the classification argument | **Verified** | https://github.com/chelseakr/fare-policy-assistant/blob/HEAD/docs/eu-ai-act-classification.md | Open-source classification doc (dated 2026-07-11) | Jul 2026 | Single system, single author; explicitly labelled as engineering assessment, not legal advice — high-quality evidence of practice, not of legal sufficiency (accessed 2026-10-10) | No contradictions found | Design systems to stay on the intended side of classification boundaries with enforced guards and eval suites, not policy promises |
| ai-act/AIACT-014 | Deployer-becomes-provider trap: rebranding, substantially modifying, or repurposing a system into high-risk use inherits the full provider obligation set (Arts 24–25); vendor compliance posture becomes part of your own | **Corroborated** | https://github.com/mchittineni/ultimate-ai-engineering-guide/blob/HEAD/ai-safety-and-governance/how-does-the-eu-ai-act-classify-ai-systems-into-risk-tiers.md ; https://medium.com/@omoboyevictor97/why-every-data-ai-team-should-care-about-the-eu-ai-act-before-2026-01c8f8c4d1e4 | Engineering guide; practitioner essay | Jul–Aug 2026 | Direct from the Act's text (Arts 24–25) (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by solicitorsjournal, europesays.com (provider/deployer assignment under Art 50) | Engineering implication: vendor due diligence is a compliance input, not a shield; contracts must assign each obligation (classification, disclosure, logging) |
| ai-act/AIACT-015 | High-risk provider requirements (Arts 9–15): risk management (Art 9, iterative lifecycle); data governance incl. bias examination (Art 10); Annex IV technical documentation (Art 11); automatic lifetime logging (Art 12); instructions for use (Art 13); human oversight (Art 14); accuracy/robustness/cybersecurity incl. data poisoning/adversarial examples/model flipping (Art 15) | **Verified** | https://github.com/open-coder-ai/chock-catalog/blob/HEAD/compliance/eu-ai-act-high-risk-triage/references/annex-iii.md | Open-source compliance triage policy (engineering mapping) | Oct 2026 | Practitioner's obligation→design-consequence map; matches the Act's text (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by fde-playbook, riftkit SKILL.md, claude-skills, suacalis/claude-skills | Each article maps to buildable controls — Arts 12 and 14 flagged by practitioners as "cheap to design in, expensive to add later" |
| ai-act/AIACT-016 | Human oversight (Art 14): design for understanding, intervention, override incl. stop control; automation-bias countermeasures (14(4)(b)); four-eyes verification for Annex III(1)(a) biometric ID (14(5)); stacks with GDPR Art 22 (system obligation vs data-subject right) | **Corroborated** | https://www.warrant.build/blog/eu-ai-act-article-14-human-oversight/ | Regulatory-analysis function (Warrant Compliance) | Jul 2026 | Annotated editorial of the Regulation's text with evidence-field mapping; explicitly not legal advice (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by chock-catalog, fde-playbook, glassbox-protocol | Regulator evidence expectation: design record + Art 13 instructions + runtime logs proving oversight was exercised per decision; build override/stop into the API, not just the UI |
| ai-act/AIACT-017 | Logging implementation pattern: append-only, tamper-evident (chained-hash) audit logs at the decision boundary capturing inputs, outputs, model+prompt versions, timestamps, confidence scores, human-override events; deployer retention ≥6 months (Art 26(6)) | **Verified** (practice) | https://github.com/pavanbhide1211/eu-ai-act-compliant-stack | Open-source compliance reference stack (article→code map) | May 2026 | Reference implementation quality — demonstrates the pattern, not production scale; SQLite demo store (accessed 2026-10-10) | Corroborated by glassbox-protocol (AIDR logging), opencomplai evidence-vault, fde-playbook | Decouple the compliance event log from application logs: independent, append-only, positioned at the decision boundary |
| ai-act/AIACT-018 | Compliance as a state machine: controls move through satisfied / evidence_missing / evidence_stale / pending_review / waived, with freshness TTLs per evidence (e.g., bias test ages out after retraining); "are we compliant" becomes a query, not an annual archaeology project | **Verified** (practice) | https://dev.to/ythouma/i-modeled-eu-ai-act-compliance-as-a-state-machine-not-a-checkbox-list-18hb | Practitioner essay (dev.to) + opencomplai GitHub repo | Oct 2026 | Single practitioner's design; explicitly open-source — evidence of practice, strong implementation idea (accessed 2026-10-10) | Consistent with GhostDrift's "documentation without release-time evidence" failure analysis | Treat compliance evidence like cached data with invalidation: attach freshness TTLs to every artifact and recompute on retraining/deployment |

| billing/B2.1 | Canonical webhook pattern: verify signature on the raw body → atomic event-ID claim (`INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`) → return 200 on duplicates; heavy work in a queue; return 2xx fast. Three-layer model: event-ID dedup + Idempotency-Key on outbound calls + business-key composite unique constraint | Corroborated | https://champlinenterprises.com/blog/stripe-webhook-reliability-handling-failures-at-scale ; https://github.com/maajankiweb/blog/blob/HEAD/./.agents/skills/engineering-payments-billing-engineer/SKILL.md ; https://github.com/oaichu/omp-foundry/blob/HEAD/skills/web/stripe-engineering/SKILL.md | corroborating (×3 practitioner sources) / champlinenterprises; maajankiweb; oaichu | ~2026 | High | Three independent sources converge on the same three-layer/claim-first shape; Practitioner conventions, not Stripe docs verbatim | Treat the PSP as a source of signed events, not a synchronous extension of local state |
| billing/B2.2 | Signature verification is not just an HMAC check: Stripe signs HMAC-SHA256 over timestamp+payload; enforce the tolerance window (default 5 minutes) to defeat replay; any middleware that parses/re-serialises JSON before verification breaks it — read the raw body first | Corroborated | https://github.com/marttp/tpcoder-portfolio/blob/HEAD/src/content/blog/payment-backend-stripe-integration-en.mdx ; https://dev.to/benjy33000/idempotent-billing-webhooks-exactly-once-fulfilment-with-stripe-44jb | practitioner (×2) / marttp; benjy33000 (dev.to) | ~2026 / Oct 2026 | Medium-High | Both sources independently stress raw-body-first + tolerance window | Webhook route must be exempt from body-parsing middleware |
| billing/B2.3 | Stripe retries non-2xx webhook deliveries for **up to 3 days**; slow endpoints (Stripe API call or email inside the handler) time out while the first attempt is *still running* — concurrent duplicate deliveries are normal | Corroborated | https://medium.com/@boukatayamed1/the-3-lines-of-stripe-webhook-code-that-will-eventually-cost-you-money-337ca86a2e44 | practitioner (commercial — sells a webhook kit) / BK Nova (Medium) | Sep 2026 | Medium | 3-day retry window matches B1.6's incident reconstruction; race-condition warning consistent with atomic-claim guidance; Commercial interest (sells a kit); illustrative | Handlers must be re-entrant-safe *and* fast; dedupe on unique constraint, not check-then-insert |
| billing/B2.4 | Three failure modes: double fulfilment, lost fulfilment (timeout after partial write; retry crashes on half-written state), trusting the payload (signature proves who sent it, not that the world still looks like the payload) | Corroborated | https://dev.to/benjy33000/idempotent-billing-webhooks-exactly-once-fulfilment-with-stripe-44jb | practitioner / benjy33000 (dev.to) | Oct 2026 | Medium | Matches B2.1/B2.3 failure taxonomy; Single source for the three-way framing | Re-fetch authoritative state; design handlers for half-written recovery |

| billing/B10.1 | HN thread "Billing systems are a nightmare for engineers": sign-error in an overdue-billing adjustment *increased* the balance by the amount paid instead of lowering it → amounts doubled daily for a customer whose card was maxed out; consensus that billing is "harder than it looks" | Corroborated | https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=31424450 | community observations / Hacker News | 2022 | Medium | Sign-error class matches B1.9's small-amount pathology; community consensus on billing difficulty; Anecdotal, 2022 | Billing arithmetic needs property-based tests (balance must never increase on payment) |
| billing/B10.2 | Stripe Connect architecture lesson: money must never touch the platform's own account (money-transmitter licensing); direct charges on connected accounts; **two separate webhook endpoints** (own billing vs connected accounts) to keep signature secrets and event routing sane | Verified | https://dev.to/hao_xu_ddc9d6fcd6b350a703/what-i-learned-building-my-first-saas-invoice-tracking-stripe-connect-private-payment-links-and-5524 | first-hand practitioner / hao_xu (dev.to) | Oct 2026 | Medium | First-hand build retrospective; Anecdotal | Marketplace billing needs endpoint-per-concern webhook design |
| billing/B10.3 | Billing test matrix: test subscriptions, invoices, webhooks, and usage-based billing as a connected graph (invoice wrong → check subscription+prices; app not updated → check webhook; usage charges wrong → check meter events); use `stripe trigger` / test clocks; test duplicate delivery, invalid signature, stale events, partial failure | Corroborated | https://medium.com/@qunoot-ahmed/how-to-test-stripe-a-qa-guide-to-subscriptions-invoices-webhooks-and-usage-based-billing-52b199e6fb74 ; https://github.com/oaichu/omp-foundry/blob/HEAD/skills/web/stripe-engineering/SKILL.md | practitioner (×2) / Qunoot Ahmed (Medium); oaichu | Sep 2026 / ~2026 | Medium | Both converge on the connected-graph + duplicate/signature/stale/partial matrix | Billing QA is state-machine testing, not endpoint testing |
| billing/B10.4 | Money amounts: integer minor units with explicit ISO 4217 currency everywhere; never floats — appears as a rule in every ledger, billing-skill, and integration source surveyed | Corroborated | (cross-source convention) https://github.com/prateekkk26/engineering-map/blob/HEAD/docs/system-design/classic-problems/design-a-payment-ledger.md ; https://github.com/maajankiweb/blog/blob/HEAD/./.agents/skills/engineering-payments-billing-engineer/SKILL.md | corroborating / prateekkk26; maajankiweb | ~2026 | High | Universal across all sources surveyed — no contradictions found | Linters should reject float money types |

| finops/F01 | FinOps Foundation definition and 6 principles**: FinOps is "an evolving cloud financial management discipline and cultural practice that enables organizations to get maximum business value by helping engineering, finance, technology and business teams to collaborate on data-driven spending decisions." Six principles: teams collaborate; everyone owns their usage; centralized FinOps function drives best practices; data accessible/timely/accurate; business value drives decisions; exploit the variable-cost model. | Verified | https://www.finops.org/framework/ (canon); corroborating mirrors: https://github.com/brencon/clipsy/blob/HEAD/.claude/skills/cfo/references/finops-framework.md, https://github.com/hacrex/cloud-finops-playbook/blob/HEAD/foundations/finops-basics.md | primary technical (the Foundation's own canon) · Org: FinOps Foundation (Linux Foundation) · Pub: ongoing · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** · Corroboration: repeated consistently across practitioner playbooks, AWS/Azure Well-Architected cost pillars · Contradictions: none on the text; tension between "centralized function" and "everyone owns" noted in practitioner postmortems |  | the canon is the shared vocabulary; quote it, then watch how practice deviates from it. |
| finops/F02 | Inform → Optimize → Operate lifecycle; Crawl → Walk → Run maturity**: three-phase continuous lifecycle (Inform: visibility/allocation; Optimize: rate+usage; Operate: governance/automation); three maturity levels Crawl/Walk/Run. | Verified | https://www.finops.org/framework/ · https://github.com/ricmmartins/azure-governance-made-simple/blob/HEAD/guide/part-5-cost-finops/ch18-finops.md | primary technical / corroborating · Org: FinOps Foundation · Pub: ongoing · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (as the published framework) · Corroboration: mirrored in AWS Well-Architected cost pillar (COST 1–5) and Azure guidance |  | lifecycle ordering matters — chargeback-style accountability attempted before Inform completes is the canonical failure sequence. |
| finops/F03 | FOCUS 1.0: vendor-neutral billing-data standard**: FinOps Open Cost and Usage Specification 1.0 standardizes cloud billing data (column names, definitions, semantics) so one query/dashboard works across providers. AWS (via CUR 2.0), Azure (Cost Management exports), Google Cloud, Oracle generate FOCUS-formatted exports and sit on the steering committee. | Verified | https://siliconangle.com/2024/06/20/finops-foundation-debut-focus-specification-unified-cloud-computing-bills/ · https://cloudfinopskit.com/insights/what-is-focus-finops.html · https://brainagents.ai/blog/what-is-focus-finops-standard | primary technical (Foundation announcement) / secondary interpretation · Pub: Jun 2024 (1.0); support updates through 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (spec exists; hyperscaler support announced) · Corroboration: consistent across explainer sources · Contradictions/limits: coverage and data quality vary by provider (GCP native export has lagged); SaaS-vendor coverage uneven — validate before migrating downstream consumers |  | multi-cloud FinOps can collapse N ETL pipelines into one — but FOCUS normalizes the schema, not the allocation policy or ownership model. |
| finops/F04 | Scopes expand beyond public cloud ("Cloud+")**: FinOps practice now formally includes SaaS, licensing, private cloud/data centre, and AI spend. State of FinOps 2025: 65% of orgs include SaaS, 49% licensing, 39% private cloud, 36% data centers. | Corroborated | https://www.webpronews.com/finops-surge-cloud-costs-hit-27-billion-by-2030-amid-ai-boom/ · https://www.channeldive.com/news/finops-teams-gain-clout-ai-costs-climb/812895/ | secondary interpretation of Foundation survey · Pub: 2025–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (survey data, vendor-reported) · Limitations: percentages are practitioner-survey self-reports, not audited adoption |  | the discipline is broadening exactly as AI spend breaks old cloud-only tooling — but standards (e.g. FOCUS for SaaS) lag the ambition. |
| finops/F05 | 2026 Framework adds Executive Strategy Alignment**: the 2026 FinOps Framework update added Executive Strategy Alignment as a formal capability: the most common failure mode is no C-suite sponsor, i.e. practitioners can see waste but lack authority to change the decisions behind it. | Corroborated | https://www.shankuehn.io/post/building-a-finops-program-diagnosis-to-operating-rhythm | practitioner (FinOps consultant) · Pub: ~Sep 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (single practitioner source; consistent with State of FinOps findings on org alignment) · Limitations: one source; the framework text itself not independently re-verified in this pass |  | diagnose sponsorship first; a FinOps program without an executive owner is an observability dashboard with no actuator. --- ## Forensic cases |
| finops/F06 | Prime Video: >90% cut, serverless → EC2/ECS (one monitoring tool)**: a Prime Video audio/video quality-monitoring service moved from Step Functions + Lambda + S3 (per-transition pricing hit scaling limits at ~5% of expected load; costly tier-1 S3 calls) to a single-process design on EC2/ECS with in-memory frame transfer: infrastructure cost down >90%, scaling headroom up, plus EC2 Compute Savings Plan eligibility. | Verified | https://www.webpronews.com/amazons-prime-video-pivots-away-from-aws-microservices-architecture/ · https://www.thestack.technology/amazon-prime-video-microservices-monolith/ · https://www.devclass.com/ci-cd/2023/05/05/reduce-costs-by-90-by-moving-from-microservices-to-monolith-amazon-internal-case-study-raises-eyebrows/1621790?td=rt-3a · https://Dev.To/aws-heroes/is-serverless-overpriced-what-can-we-learn-from-the-primevideo-team-1d9i · https://www.networkworld.com/article/972298/6-lessons-from-the-amazon-prime-video-serverless-vs-monolith-flap.html | first-hand practitioner (company engineering blog, via press) / corroborating · Org: Amazon Prime Video (Marcin Kolny) · Pub: May 2023 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (the 90% figure and architecture details are the company's own published account) · Corroboration: five independent outlets report the same facts · Contradictions: none on facts; narrative misreadings ("serverless is dead", "cloud exit") corrected by balanced analyses |  | per-invocation pricing × high-frequency workloads = the crossover pattern; the lesson is evolutionary architecture, not anti-serverless. Scope: ONE tool, ONE team, still on AWS — not a repatriation. |
| finops/F07 | 37signals: $3.2M → under $1M/year, full AWS exit**: 2022 AWS spend $3.2M ($2.3M on commodity servers); ~$700K Dell hardware recouped from H2-2023 savings; monthly spend $180K → $80K pre-exit; S3 exit: $1.5M/year bill replaced by $1.5M one-time 18 PB Pure Storage arrays (<$200K/year to run), with AWS waiving $250K egress under its free-exit-transfer policy. Total: $3.2M/year → "well under a million", same headcount, delete-AWS-account planned summer 2025. | Verified | https://www.theregister.com/special-features/2025/05/09/37signals-on-prem-migration-to-save-millions-abandon-aws/691340 · https://www.itpro.com/cloud/cloud-computing/this-software-company-says-ditching-the-cloud-was-worth-usd10-million-in-savings-and-theres-still-more-to-come · https://www.thestack.technology/dhh-aws-egress-s3-pure/ · https://www.itpro.com/cloud/370111/37-signals-save-7m-abandoning-cloud-prices-grotesque | first-hand practitioner (CTO's dated, numerically specific posts) / press corroboration · Org: 37signals (David Heinemeier Hansson) · Pub: 2023–2025 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (company's own detailed accounting, consistently reported) · Contradictions/limits: savings vs a projected counterfactual bill (modelled, not measured); generalisability limited (mature Rails apps, flat traffic, experienced ops, ideological CTO); repatriation is single-digit % of companies per analyst; egress-waiver policy eased the exit and can change |  | repatriation can pay at this shape of company; it is not a general prescription. Vendor figures direction-trusted, magnitude-discounted applies to the company's own counterfactual projections too. |
| finops/F08 | Dropbox: $74.6M over two years (S-1 documented)**: Infrastructure Optimization initiative (S3 → custom colo infra, "Magic Pocket"): $92.5M decrease in third-party datacentre expense in 2016 offset by $53M in depreciation/facilities/support = $39.5M net 2016 + $35.1M 2017 = $74.6M over two years. Cost of revenue down 4% then 6% while revenue grew 31%. Metadata was always private-DC; ~10% of data stayed on AWS; dead-user data deletion bundled into the same initiative. | Verified | https://www.geekwire.com/2018/dropbox-saved-almost-75-million-two-years-building-tech-infrastructure/?ref=runtime.news · https://www.datacenterknowledge.com/cloud/here-s-how-much-money-dropbox-saved-by-moving-out-of-the-cloud · https://www.techtarget.com/it-infrastructure/blog/AWS-Cloud-Cover/Dropbox-is-likely-an-outlier-with-its-successful-cloud-data-migration-off-AWS | primary technical (SEC S-1 filing) via press · Org: Dropbox · Pub: Feb 2018 (S-1) · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (SEC filing — strongest evidence class in this file) · Corroboration: multiple outlets, same numbers · Contradictions/limits: bundled waste deletion inflates the headline; $53M first-year capex; partial exit; commentators called it an outlier requiring Dropbox's scale and uniform workload |  | the gold-standard documented repatriation; the fine print (partial exit, bundled deletion, capex) is what makes it instructive. |
| finops/F09 | Segment: >$1M cut via billing forensics, stayed on cloud**: AWS bill grew to two-thirds of COGS with no matching employee usage growth; two engineers, 2–3 months of forensic work, cut >$1M. Runaway employee use was not the cause. | Verified | https://www.informationweek.com/data-management/inscrutable-amazon-bill-behind-it-a-1-million-savings | first-hand practitioner (company blog via press) · Org: Segment (CTO Calvin Frank-Owen) · Pub: ~2017–18 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (company's own account) · Limitations: single source; dollar figure described as "over a million", significance asserted not audited |  | the neglected lesson — understanding the bill, not leaving the cloud, was the fix. Standing bill-forensics capability beats fire drills. |
| finops/F10 | Spotify: Cost Insights in Backstage, gamified showback**: Spotify built Cost Insights into its Backstage developer portal (engineer-visible cloud costs + optimization suggestions) plus an internal crowdsourced wiki ("Our Cookbook"); developers treated optimization "like a game"; a central cost-management org intervened only on anomalous ramps; millions in annual savings reported. Anomaly detection was on the roadmap at reporting time. | Verified | https://www.infoworld.com/article/2267139/how-5-companies-got-their-developers-to-care-about-cloud-costs.html · https://siliconangle.com/2020/11/20/spotify-scales-infrastructure-thousands-microservices-open-source-fail-faster-approach-kubecon/ · https://dev.to/castai/zoom-spotify-and-others-slashed-their-cloud-costs-by-millions-how-did-they-do-it-38b | first-hand practitioner (Spotify engineering, via press/analyst) · Org: Spotify · Pub: 2020–2021 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (Spotify's own account of the mechanism) · Limitations: dollar savings company-reported and unspecified — direction-trusted, magnitude-discounted; single-source per outlet |  | the reference implementation of showback + social incentives + targeted central intervention — notably not chargeback. |
| finops/F11 | Uber (2026): AI cost held flat while agent requests grew 9.4×**: live model routing (Pareto-optimal picks), defaulting subagents to cheaper models, batching multiple tool calls into scripts → total AI spend roughly flat Feb–Aug 2026 while weekly agent requests grew 9.4×; cost per session down 52% from June peak. | Corroborated | https://dev.to/topuzas/the-real-cost-curve-of-running-agents-in-production-from-four-different-companies-9jf | practitioner case-study write-up · Pub: Oct 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (detailed write-up; company-original sourcing not independently verified — single-source, direction-trusted, magnitude-discounted) |  | sharpest public example of cost-aware model routing as engineering architecture rather than a finance exercise. |
| finops/F12 | GEICO: enterprise repatriation after 2.5× bill growth**: after a decade-long cloud journey, bills up 2.5× with worse reliability; repatriating workloads as part of an architectural overhaul. | Corroborated | https://thenewstack.io/why-companies-are-ditching-the-cloud-the-rise-of-cloud-repatriation/ | first-hand practitioner (VP Platform & Infrastructure Engineering Rebecca Weekly, via press) · Pub: ~2024 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (executive quote, single outlet) · Limitations: no published dollar figures or architecture detail |  | enterprise-scale repatriation driven by architecture (cost + reliability together), not ideology. --- ## Failure evidence (why compulsory) |
| finops/F13 | Segment's two-thirds-of-COGS AWS bill**: | Verified |  |  |  | **Verified** · Implication: margin collapse from unmetered costs is a documented, not hypothetical, failure mode. |  |  |
| finops/F14 | CloudWatch logging: $2,100/mo → $340/mo in one afternoon**: 800-user product, $11,400/mo total AWS; CloudWatch Logs $2,100/mo — 43 log groups, no retention policies, one ingesting 180 GB/mo of debug-level logging left on for eight months. Fix: retention policies, kill debug, delete dead groups → $340/mo. | Corroborated | https://dev.to/vlad_z_16b6320e21f32bee0d/an-11400-aws-bill-and-cloudwatch-logs-was-2100-of-it-37aj | first-hand practitioner · Pub: Oct 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (detailed first-hand account; the debug-logging pattern is independently ubiquitous) · Limitations: single account, self-reported |  | observability spend is a shadow second bill; retention and log levels belong in cost review, not just engineering hygiene. |
| finops/F15 | Lambda/Fargate crossover: $12,400/mo vs $3,200/mo flat**: same REST API on Lambda and ECS Fargate for 3 months: Lambda $87/mo at low traffic → $12,400/mo at high (30M req/mo; API Gateway and CloudWatch the hidden multipliers); Fargate ~$3,200 flat; crossover ~3.2M requests/mo; hybrid cut the bill 53–68%. | Corroborated | https://dev.to/garrett_yan/serverless-vs-containers-a-cost-analysis-with-real-numbers-3dao | first-hand practitioner (measured experiment) · Pub: Sep 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (independent second account reports a $12,000/mo Lambda bill rebuilt on EC2 for ~$315: https://medium.com/@webdeveloper45/our-serverless-lambda-bill-hit-12-000-we-switched-back-to-ec2-for-400-heres-what-changed-9939039071e8) · Limitations: both single-source; your crossover point is your own — direction trusted, magnitudes discounted |  | per-request pricing reverses above a crossover point; the crossover is measurable and should be. |
| finops/F16 | Runaway AI agent: ~$50K in under an hour (Mandiant)**: an accounting agent in a runaway loop made 15,000+ high-cost API calls in under an hour → ~$50,000 cloud charges, no attacker involved. Root causes: no spend ceiling, no owner with a kill switch, cost signals arriving hours after spend. | Verified | https://www.shashi.co/2026/10/mandiant-says-one-runaway-ai-agent.html (reporting Mandiant/Google AI Risk and Resilience report, Sep 16 2026) | primary technical (Mandiant/Google report) via press · Pub: Sep–Oct 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (reported from the Mandiant/Google report) · Corroboration: consistent with practitioner analyses of agent cost-failure modes (unbounded retries, no token budgets, delayed observability) |  | cost controls must live in the orchestration layer — budgets, hard stops, per-run ceilings — because billing-latency detection is too slow for agent-speed spend. |
| finops/F17 | Agentic token multiplier: 5–30× chatbot consumption, mostly invisible**: agentic workflows burn 5–30× more tokens than chatbots (Gartner); 50–90% of agentic consumption is invisible internal traffic (repeated system prompts, reasoning loops, context re-injection). One case: ~€5 → ~€80 per operation after uninstrumented agentic automation. | Corroborated | https://github.com/andresilvalab/ai-ops-sec/blob/HEAD/src/content/posts/en/crise-dos-tokens.md · https://www.techaheadcorp.com/blog/inference-cost-explosion/ | practitioner synthesis citing Gartner/analyst figures · Pub: 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (multiple sources, same structure) · Limitations: multipliers are analyst/practitioner estimates, not controlled measurements — direction-trusted, magnitude-discounted |  | instrument tokens per run and isolate internal agent traffic before optimizing; the meter you watch (user tokens) is not the meter that bills you. |
| finops/F18 | AI cost crisis structure: prices fell, bills multiplied**: per-token prices collapsed (Stanford AI Index 2025: inference costs down ~280× in two years; GPT-4-class from $30/MTok input 2023 to ~$0.10–0.40 2026); simultaneously Deloitte Q4 2025: enterprise AI gross margins 40% → 33%; Gartner: 40% of AI agent projects cancelled by 2027 on cost overruns; Uber burned its annual AI budget in ~four months (CTO public admission). | Corroborated | https://medium.com/@horecny/the-ai-price-collapse-is-real-your-excuse-to-wait-is-not-cc575844497c · https://www.techaheadcorp.com/blog/inference-cost-explosion/ · https://github.com/andresilvalab/ai-ops-sec/blob/HEAD/src/content/posts/en/crise-dos-tokens.md | secondary interpretation / analyst citations · Pub: 2025–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** on the paradox structure; the Gartner 40% cancellation figure is widely repeated but single-analyst-sourced — direction-trusted, magnitude-discounted |  | unit-price trends mislead; product cost is tokens-per-task × tasks, and agentic tasks moved the first factor against the price trend. --- ## Practices |
| finops/F19 | Showback vs chargeback consensus: start with showback**: practitioner consensus — start with showback (awareness), graduate to chargeback (accountability) only when attribution is defensible; chargeback on unreliable attribution "turns every invoice into a political dispute"; never charge teams for spend they cannot influence. FinOps Foundation: neither model is inherently more mature. | Corroborated | https://dev.to/zop_8abedcc7e12/chargeback-vs-showback-building-team-level-cloud-cost-accountability-330h · https://www.finout.io/blog/cloud-cost-allocation-basics-benefits-and-types · https://www.aiplusinfo.com/blog/finops-for-agentic-ai/ · https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/finops-ai-chargeback-actually-works-sowdesh-kumar-sukumar-y7hjc | practitioner guidance / community observations · Pub: 2025–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (four+ independent sources, consistent rules) · Limitations: behavior-change rates quoted (10–20% showback, 40–60% chargeback) are one practitioner's measured claim, not controlled studies |  | chargeback is a policy decision with data preconditions (~95% attribution, published shared-cost rules, controllable levers) — not a maturity badge. |
| finops/F20 | Only 14% of orgs run active chargeback (State of FinOps 2025)**: 14% of organizations implement active chargeback programs; showback-first is the norm. Survey: 861 respondents, $69B combined annual cloud spend. | Corroborated | https://cast.ai/blog/kubernetes-chargeback-showback/ (citing FinOps Foundation 2025 State of FinOps) | secondary interpretation of Foundation survey · Pub: 2025 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (survey data via a second source) · Limitations: self-reported survey; definition of "active chargeback" per respondents |  | chargeback is the exception, not the rule — programs should be designed for the showback reality. |
| finops/F21 | Top priorities: waste reduction #1, governance rising (State of FinOps 2025)**: workload optimization and waste reduction was the #1 practitioner priority (50%); allocation and forecasting next; in forward priorities, governance-and-policy moved to #1 while waste reduction stayed #2; unit economics and managing AI/ML spend rose sharply. | Corroborated | https://inverodigital.com/insights/2025-state-of-finops-report-highlights/ · https://www.webpronews.com/finops-surge-cloud-costs-hit-27-billion-by-2030-amid-ai-boom/ | secondary interpretation of Foundation survey · Pub: 2025 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (two independent summaries, same numbers) · Limitations: survey self-report |  | the field is maturing from "find waste" to "govern at scale" — exactly the bottleneck sequence in §6 of the main file. |
| finops/F22 | Anomaly-detection operating pattern (multi-scope, dollar floors, owner routing)**: monitors at multiple scopes (service, account, tag) simultaneously; absolute-dollar thresholds alongside percentages; alerts to FinOps AND the owning team; monthly false-positive tuning. Aggregate-only monitors miss masked anomalies. | Corroborated | https://github.com/optimnow/cloud-finops-skills (finops-anomaly-management.md; URL entry unavailable in search output — text only) · corroborating pattern in https://github.com/azibat92/finops-sentinel- (z-score, 30-day window, severity tiers) | practitioner guidance / open-source implementations · Pub: 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (consistent pattern across guidance + multiple OSS implementations) · Limitations: each deployment's effectiveness is self-reported; no controlled comparison of methods |  | anomaly detection is table stakes and cheap (native tooling is free) — the differentiator is routing and tuning discipline, not the algorithm. |
| finops/F23 | Cloud waste estimated at 28–32%; Kubernetes CPU utilization ~8%**: ~28–32% of cloud spend estimated wasted (Flexera surveys); Cast AI 2026: average production Kubernetes CPU utilization ~8%, CPU overprovisioning ~69%, memory overprovisioning ~79%. | Corroborated | https://www.opentext.com/fr/media/point-of-view/optimize-cloud-spend-and-business-value-with-finops-pov-en.pdf (citing Flexera 2023–2025) · https://tech-insider.org/devzero-vs-kubecost-vs-cast-ai-2026/ (citing Cast AI 2026) | vendor surveys/reports · Pub: 2023–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** as survey/vendor-reported figures · Limitations: estimates and vendor-sampled measurements — direction-trusted, magnitude-discounted |  | the waste pool is large enough that Inform-phase work pays before any architectural bets. |
| finops/F24 | Idle vs inefficient: different buckets, different fixes**: idle (zero-traffic) workloads and inefficient (low-utilization) workloads need different fixes — rightsizing an idle pod improves its efficiency dashboard while the correct action is deletion or scale-to-zero; rightsizing can hide idle spend. | Corroborated | https://devtocash.com/blog/2026-09-16-kubernetes-idle-cost-find-zero-traffic-workloads-scale-to-zero | first-hand practitioner · Pub: Sep 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (consistent with waste-class taxonomies across sources) · Limitations: single practitioner's cluster experience |  | track idle and inefficient as separate waste classes with separate detectors and separate remediation. |
| finops/F25 | Waste classes and their mechanical detection rules**: unattached EBS (status=available 30+ days, gp3 $0.08/GB-mo), idle ALB/NLB (zero requests 14 days, ~$16.43/mo base), orphaned snapshots ($0.05/GB-mo), unassociated Elastic IPs (~$3.65/mo), stopped instances still paying for volumes, NAT gateways with zero traffic ($32.85/mo). | Corroborated | https://devtocash.com/blog/2026-09-01-aws-waste-reclamation-ai-agent-unattached-ebs-idle-load-balancers · https://github.com/ellevas/cloudrift · https://github.com/nathanl05/aws-cost-analyzer | practitioner / open-source tooling · Pub: 2025–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (consistent rules across independent tools and write-ups; prices are us-east-1 list) · Limitations: list prices, not any org's effective rates |  | waste reclamation is mechanical and automatable — the detection rules are stable; the gap is enforcement (dry-run → notify → auto-remediate). |
| finops/F26 | Commitment purchasing on guesswork (55%)**: 55% of developers say cloud purchasing commitments are "ultimately based on guesswork"; 55% say it's too hard to understand their cloud spending. | Corroborated | https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/6222ca42ea87e1bd1aa1d10c/67be20d4204f8f764a4410fa_FinOps%20in%20Focus%20Report.pdf | industry survey report · Pub: 2025 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (survey data; consistent with practitioner complaints) · Limitations: self-reported; vendor-adjacent publication |  | the largest discount lever (30–70% via commitments) is operated on vibes — commitment management must be an engineering input (only engineering knows what's steady-state), not a finance purchase. |
| finops/F27 | Unit-economics denominator discipline**: track physical denominators (requests, tokens, orders, GB) for engineering targets and commercial denominators (per tenant, % of revenue) for margin — keep both; report total spend next to the unit every time; the denominator must already be counted and owned by the business, or it will be disputed. | Corroborated | https://www.anystackengineering.com/blog/cloud-unit-economics-cost-per-request-for-engineering-leaders · https://github.com/ankitjha67/product-architect/blob/HEAD/agents/68-finops-cloud-economics.md · https://www.ai-infra-link.com/why-engineering-teams-should-own-cloud-costs-in-2026/ | practitioner guidance · Pub: 2025–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (three independent sources, same rules) · Limitations: practitioner consensus, not measured outcomes |  | unit economics is the management signal that separates growth from waste; total spend alone cannot do that. |
| finops/F28 | OpenCost: CNCF incubating, FinOps-certified K8s allocation**: OpenCost is the CNCF vendor-neutral project (incubating since Oct 2024) for measuring and allocating Kubernetes/cloud costs; joins K8s API + Prometheus + cloud billing; allocates by namespace/workload/pod/label; without billing wired in, reports public on-demand list prices, not your rates; FinOps Foundation certified allocation methodology. | Verified | http://cast.ai/blog/opencost-kubernetes/ · https://horovits.medium.com/open-standards-in-finops-mastering-kubernetes-cost-and-bill-efb00731e99a?responsesOpen=true · https://github.com/lurodrisilva/personal-skills/blob/HEAD/platform-engineering/opencost/SKILL.md | primary technical (project/spec) / corroborating · Pub: 2022–2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Verified** (project status and architecture from primary sources) · Limitations: none material |  | the neutral measurement plane for Kubernetes cost; everything downstream (chargeback, rightsizing) inherits its pricing-source trust. |
| finops/F29 | Kubernetes idle-headroom ownership is where chargeback stalls**: pod→namespace allocation mechanics are solved; who owns idle headroom is organizational, and most chargeback programs stall on it — unresolved, the first dispute kills the program; unallocated cost above ~5% makes reports disputable. | Corroborated | https://cast.ai/blog/kubernetes-chargeback-showback/ | practitioner (vendor) guide · Pub: Sep 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Corroborated** (consistent with consultant postmortems on chargeback failure) · Limitations: single detailed source; vendor-authored |  | answer "who owns idle" in policy before going live; treat idle as a platform-team line item rather than taxing whoever was scheduled next to it. |
| finops/F30 | Per-agent AI attribution at the gateway (proposed pattern)**: every model call should carry team/use-case/environment metadata applied at a gateway choke point; shared costs (gateway, evals, guardrails) need an explicit documented allocation policy; metering must be auditable by a skeptical engineer; pilots get capped innovation allowances exempt from chargeback. | Proposed | https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/finops-ai-chargeback-actually-works-sowdesh-kumar-sukumar-y7hjc · https://www.aiplusinfo.com/blog/finops-for-agentic-ai/ | practitioner proposals · Pub: Oct 2026 · Accessed: 2026-10-10 |  | **Proposed** (reference designs, not measured programs) · Corroboration: consistent with Uber's routing approach (F11) as direction |  | AI spend needs its own attribution architecture — namespace/tag models don't fit token flows; build the gateway before the chargeback. |
